The Grothendieck construction in the context of tangent categories
Marcello Lanfranchi

Abstract
The Grothendieck construction establishes an equivalence between fibrations, a.k.a. fibred categories, and indexed categories, and is one of the fundamental results of category theory. Cockett and Cruttwell introduced the notion of fibrations into the context of tangent categories and proved that the fibres of a tangent fibration inherit a tangent structure from the total tangent category. The main goal of this paper is to provide a Grothendieck construction for tangent fibrations. Our first attempt will focus on providing a correspondence between tangent fibrations and indexed tangent categories, which are collections of tangent categories and tangent morphisms indexed by the objects and morphisms of a base tangent category. We will show that this construction inverts Cockett and Cruttwell’s result but it does not provide a full equivalence between these two concepts. In order to understand how to define a genuine Grothendieck equivalence in the context of tangent categories, inspired by Street’s formal approach to monad theory we introduce a new concept: tangent objects. We show that tangent fibrations arise as tangent objects of a suitable 2 2 -category and we employ this characterization to lift the Grothendieck construction between fibrations and indexed categories to a genuine Grothendieck equivalence between tangent fibrations and tangent indexed categories.



Acknowledgements
.
We would like to thank Geoffrey Cruttwell for introducing us to the world of fibrations. We also would like to thank Dorette Pronk and Geoffrey Vooys for the useful discussions on this topic and for the great suggestions for future work. We would like to thank again Geoffrey Cruttwell and Dorette Pronk for their support, suggestions, and corrections, and Rory Lucyshyn-Wright for an important correction in the definition of tangent objects (Remark
4.2
). We are also grateful to the anonymous referees for their suggestions.

1
Introduction

In topology, the Fibre Bundle Construction Theorem establishes an equivalence between fibre bundles on a given base space and a collection of fibres, equipped with a suitable family of transition functions (cf.
[
Ste99
, Section 3]
). The category-theoretic analog of the Fibre Bundle Construction is captured by the Grothendieck construction, which establishes an equivalence between (cloven) fibrations, also known as fibred categories, and indexed categories. Informally, the latter can be interpreted as a collection of categories indexed by the objects of a fixed category, together with a collection of functors between them, indexed by the morphisms of the index category, in a compatible way with the indexing of the objects, while the former is the category-theoretic analog of fibre bundles.

More concretely, a fibration consists of a functor from a category, known as the total category of the fibration, to another category, known as the base category of the fibration, satisfying a universal property for which morphisms of the base category can be lifted to the total category suitably. This universal property of fibrations is in every aspect the category-theoretic analog of the path-lifting property of the universal covering space of a topological space.

Fibrations are suitable to describe objects that depend on another object of another category. An example of a fibration is given by the category of pairs
(R,M)
formed by a ring
R
and an
R
-module
M
, and morphisms
(f,\xi)\colon(R,M)\to(S,N)
defined by a morphism
f\colon R\to S
of rings and a morphism of
R
-modules
\xi\colon M\to f^{\ast}N
, where
f^{\ast}N
denotes the
R
-module induced by
f\colon R\to S
via scalar inclusion. The fibration corresponds to the projection which sends each pair
(R,M)
to the underlying ring
R
and each morphism
(f,\xi)
to the underlying morphism of rings
f
.

Equivalently, one can display the same information by defining a pseudofunctor which sends each ring
R
to the category
\mathsf{MOD}_{R}
of
R
-modules and each morphism of rings
f\colon R\to S
to the functor
f^{\ast}\colon\mathsf{MOD}_{S}\to\mathsf{MOD}_{R}
. The Grothendieck construction establishes that every (cloven) fibration is equivalent to a fibration whose total category is the category of pairs
(A,E)
formed by an object
A
of the base category together with an object
E
on the fibre over
A
.

Cockett and Cruttwell in
[
CC18
, Section 5]
, introduced the notion of fibrations into the context of tangent categories. Tangent categories, first defined by Rosickỳ in
[
Ros84
]
and recently revisited and generalized by Cockett and Cruttwell in
[
CC14
]
, provide a minimal categorical framework to axiomatize the tangent bundle functor of differential geometry.

Concretely, a tangent category consists of a category
\mathbb{X}
equipped with an endofunctor
\mathrm{T}
, called the tangent bundle functor, together with a collection of natural transformations: a projection
p\colon\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}
which makes
\mathrm{T}A
into a bundle for every object
A
of
\mathbb{X}
, a zero section
z\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
of the projection, known as the zero morphism, a sum morphism
s\colon\mathrm{T}_{2}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
, whose domain is the pullback of the projection along itself, that allows one to sum tangent vectors of the same fibre, a vertical lift
l\colon\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{2}
which introduces a notion of local linearity, and a canonical flip
c\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{2}
, which axiomatizes the commutativity of partial derivatives for smooth functions.

In their paper
[
CC18
]
, Cockett and Cruttwell proved an important result: the fibres of a tangent fibration, i.e., a fibration between tangent categories which preserves the tangent structures, inherit a tangent structure from the total tangent category, strongly preserved by the substitution functors (cf.
[
CC18
, Theorem 5.3]
). One would like to see this result as part of a correspondence between tangent fibrations and indexed categories whose fibres are equipped with a tangent structure and whose substitution functors are compatible with these tangent structures, which will be called indexed tangent categories.

The first goal of this paper is to explore this idea, by showing that the Grothendieck construction of such indexed categories gives rise to a tangent fibration. We refer to this as the
reduced Grothendieck construction for tangent categories
. This, together with Cockett and Cruttwell’s construction, defines an adjunction which, however, does not provide an equivalence of categories.

Even if this “fibrewise” Grothendieck construction does not provide a full equivalence, it is still very relevant in tangent category theory. In particular, Cockett and Cruttwell have shown how the construction of the slice tangent category can be obtained by looking at the tangent structures on the fibres of a given tangent fibration. Furthermore, this construction plays a crucial role in the theory of differential bundles (cf.
[
CC18
]
).

The main goal of this paper is to achieve a genuine Grothendieck equivalence in the context of tangent categories and clarify why the reduced Grothendieck construction fails in this manner. Our approach relies on a new concept: the notion of a
tangent object
of a
2
-category. The idea is to introduce a formal approach for tangent category theory, inspired by Street’s formal approach to monad theory
[
Str72
]
. After introducing this definition, we show that tangent categories are precisely tangent objects in the
2
-category of categories. Similarly, we show that tangent fibrations are precisely tangent objects in the
2
-category of fibrations. We take advantage of this fact to lift the equivalence between (cloven) fibrations and indexed categories to a genuine Grothendieck equivalence between (cloven) tangent fibrations and tangent indexed categories.

The desire to explore a Grothendieck construction between certain fibrations and indexed categories equipped with extra structure is not new. For instance, Moeller and Vasilakopoulou in
[
MV20
]
investigated a Grothendieck construction in the context of monoidal categories. They encountered a similar phenomenon: a monoidal structure on a fibration can be either fibrewise or global, i.e., such that the total category becomes monoidal. In the general case, these two structures are not equivalent, however, under certain assumptions they become compatible.

As part of our investigation, we also compare the notion of internal fibrations introduced by Street in
[
Str74
]
with Cockett and Cruttwell’s notion of tangent fibrations. In particular, we show that tangent fibrations can be described as a suitable subclass of pseudoalgebras of a
2
-monad.

1.1
Outline

In Section
2
, we recall the notions of a fibration and an indexed category, and we extend the classical Grothendieck construction to an equivalence between fibrations and indexed categories over a non-fixed base. In Section
2.2
, we recall the main definitions of tangent category theory.

Section
3
is dedicated to recalling Cockett and Cruttwell’s notion of a tangent fibration and to reformulating their characterization of the fibres of a tangent fibration in terms of a functor which sends a tangent fibration to an indexed tangent category.

In Section
3.2
, we show that this functor admits a left adjoint, which however does not provide an equivalence of categories. We conclude Section
3
with a comparison between internal fibrations in the
2
-category of tangent categories and tangent fibrations.

In order to construct a genuine Grothendieck construction for tangent fibrations, in Section
4
we introduce a new concept: the notion of a tangent object. In Section
5
, we employ this formal approach to prove that tangent fibrations are equivalent to tangent indexed categories, i.e., tangent objects in the
2
-category of indexed categories.

Finally, Section
6
is dedicated to summarize the story of this paper and to discuss some directions for future work.

1.2
Notation

Categories are denoted by capitalized names, e.g.,
\mathsf{CAT}
for the category of (small) categories. To denote identity morphisms we adopt the notation
\mathsf{id}_{A}\colon A\to A
and for the composition of morphisms, functors, and natural transformations we adopt the functional convention, e.g.,
f\circ g\colon A\to C
, for
f\colon B\to C
and
g\colon A\to B
. However, when a composition of functors
F\circ G
is evaluated on an object
A
or on a morphism
f
, we adopt the applicative notation, e.g.,
FGA
or
FGf
to denote
(F\circ G)(A)
or
(F\circ G)(f)
, respectively.

2
Background

In this section, we recall some of the main results of fibration theory and tangent category theory. In particular, in Section
2.1
we recollect the notion of fibrations, indexed categories, and the Grothendieck construction, while in Section
2.2
we recall some of the main concepts and results of tangent category theory. For fibration theory we refer to
[
Bor94
]
and
[
Str23
]
; for tangent category theory we refer to
[
CC14
]
, and
[
CC18
]
.

2.1
Fibrations and Grothendieck construction

A fibration, introduced by Grothendieck (see
[
Gro63
, Exposé VI.12 Section 12]
), is the category-theoretic analog of a fibre bundle. Concretely, a fibration consists of a functor from a category, called the total category, to another category, called the base category, which satisfies a universal property analog to the path-lifting property of the universal covering space of a topological space. An equivalent way of understanding a fibration is to regard it as a collection of categories, called
fibres
, indexed by the objects of the base category, “glued” together.

This interpretation is made precise by the Grothendieck construction, which establishes an equivalence between (cloven) fibrations and indexed categories. Concretely, the indexed category associated with a (cloven) fibration is the pseudofunctor which sends an object of the base category of the fibration to the fibre over the given object.

In this section, we recall the definitions of a (cloven) fibration, of an indexed category, and we recall the Grothendieck construction as presented in
[
Bor94
, Theorem 8.3.1]
.

Let us briefly establish some of the jargon we adopt in the rest of the paper.

Notation 2.1
.

Given a functor
\Pi\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
, we refer to
\mathbb{X}
as the
base category
of
\Pi
and to
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
as the
total category
of
\Pi
. For an object
A
of
\mathbb{X}
, an object
E
of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
is
over

A
if
\Pi(E)=A
and similarly, a morphism
\varphi\colon E\to E^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
is
over
a morphism
f\colon A\to A^{\prime}
if
\Pi(\varphi)=f
. Equivalently, we also say that
\varphi
is a
lift
of
f
. A morphism of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
is
vertical
if it is over the identity morphism of an object of
\mathbb{X}
. The
fibre
over an object
A
of
\mathbb{X}
refers to the category
\Pi^{-1}(A)
whose objects are objects of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
over
A
and morphisms are morphisms of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
over
\mathsf{id}_{A}
.

To introduce the notion of a fibration, we first need to recall the definition of a cartesian morphism.

Definition 2.2
.

Given a functor
\Pi\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
, a morphism
\varphi\colon\tilde{E}\to E^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
over a morphism
f\colon\tilde{A}\to A^{\prime}
, is
cartesian
if for any given morphism
\psi\colon E\to E^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}
over a morphism
g\colon A\to A^{\prime}
, and any morphism
h\colon A\to\tilde{A}
of
\mathbb{X}
making the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tilde{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

there is a unique lift
\xi\colon E\to\tilde{E}
of
h
such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tilde{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

The map
\varphi
is also called a
cartesian lift
of the morphism
f
on
E^{\prime}
.

Definition 2.3
.

A
fibration
consists of a functor
\Pi\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
for which for every pair
(f,E^{\prime})
formed by a morphism
f\colon A\to A^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}
and by an object
E^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
over
A^{\prime}
, there is a cartesian lift of
f
whose codomain is
E^{\prime}
. A
cleavage
of a fibration is a choice of a cartesian lift
\varphi^{\left(f\right)}_{E^{\prime}}
for each pair
(f,E^{\prime})
formed by a morphism
f\colon A\to A^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}
and of an object
E^{\prime}
over
A^{\prime}
. Finally, a
cloven fibration
is a fibration equipped with a cleavage.

Notation 2.4
.

In the following, a fibration
\Pi\colon\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}
is denoted by
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\Pi)
. Moreover, for a cloven fibration
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\Pi)
, the cartesian lift of a morphism
f\colon A\to A^{\prime}
specified by the cleveage, corresponding to an object
E^{\prime}
over
A^{\prime}
, is denoted by:

\displaystyle\varphi^{\left(f\right)}_{E^{\prime}}\colon f^{\ast}E^{\prime}\to E^{\prime}

For the sake of simplicity, when the object
E^{\prime}
is clear by the context, we omit the subscript
{}_{E^{\prime}}
. When the symbol adopted for a cloven fibration is decorated with a subscript or with a superscript, the same decoration is applied to the cartesian lifts. For example, for a cloven fibration
(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square},\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\prime},\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\square})
, a morphism
f\colon A\to A^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}
, and an object
E^{\prime}
over
A^{\prime}
, the corresponding cartesian lift is denoted by
\varphi_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\left(f\right)}
.

Every morphism
\psi\colon E\to E^{\prime}
of the total category
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
of a fibration
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\Pi)
admits a decomposition into a vertical morphism
\xi\colon E\to\tilde{E}
and a cartesian morphism
\varphi\colon\tilde{E}\to E^{\prime}
. To see why, consider the morphism
(f\colon A\to A^{\prime})\colon\kern-4.30554pt=\Pi(\psi)
. Since
\Pi
is a fibration, there is a cartesian lift
\varphi\colon\tilde{E}\to E^{\prime}
of
f
whose codomain is
E^{\prime}
. In particular, since
\varphi
is a lift of
f
,
\Pi(\varphi)\circ\mathsf{id}_{A}=\Pi(\varphi)=f
. For the cartesian property of
\varphi
, there is a unique morphism
\xi\colon E\to\tilde{E}
of
\mathbb{X}
such that
\Pi(\xi)=\mathsf{id}_{A}
and
\varphi\circ\xi=\psi
. Since
\xi
is a lift of
\mathsf{id}_{A}
,
\xi
is vertical.

This decomposition is not unique, since there could be more than one cartesian lift of the morphism
f
. However, when the fibration is equipped with a cleavage, there is a canonical choice of such a decomposition. In particular, for a cloven fibration, each morphism
\psi\colon E\to E^{\prime}
of the total category can be decomposed as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\ast}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{\left(f\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(f\right)}}

However, since the cartesian lift
\varphi^{\left(f\right)}\colon f^{\ast}E^{\prime}\to E^{\prime}
is determined by the cleavage, by
f
, and by
E^{\prime}
, each morphism
\psi\colon E\to E^{\prime}
of the total category is fully specified by a pair
(f,\xi^{\left(f\right)})
formed by a morphism
f\colon\kern-4.30554pt=\Pi(\psi)
of
\mathbb{X}
and by a vertical morphism
\xi^{\left(f\right)}\colon E\to f^{\ast}E^{\prime}
. This is precisely the intuition underpinning the Grothendieck construction.

The first step is to “split” a fibration into the collection of its fibres. Such a collection has the structure of an indexed category. Informally, an indexed category on a category
\mathbb{X}
consists of a collection of categories indexed by the objects of
\mathbb{X}
together with a collection of functors between these categories, indexed by the morphisms of
\mathbb{X}
, and for which the indexing of the categories and of the functors are compatible with the composition and the identity morphisms of
\mathbb{X}
.

Definition 2.5
.

An
indexed category
on a category
\mathbb{X}
is a pseudofunctor
\mathfrak{I}\colon\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
, where
\mathsf{CAT}
denotes the
2
-category of categories. Concretely, this consists of the following data:

categories.

For each object
A
of
\mathbb{X}
, a category
\mathbb{X}^{\left(A\right)}
;

substitution functors.

for each morphism
f\colon A\to A^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}
, a functor
f^{\ast}\colon\mathbb{X}^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}\to\mathbb{X}^{\left(A\right)}
, called a
substitution functor
;

unitor.

For each object
A
of
\mathbb{X}
, a natural isomorphism
\mathfrak{I}^{0}\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}^{\left(A\right)}}\cong\mathsf{id}_{A}^{\ast}
, called the
unitor
;

compositor.

For each pair of composable morphisms
f\colon A\to A^{\prime}
and
g\colon B\to C
of
\mathbb{X}
, a natural isomorphism
\mathfrak{I}^{2}\colon f^{\ast}\circ g^{\ast}\cong(g\circ f)^{\ast}
, called the
compositor
;

satisfying some compatibility conditions. We invite the reader to consult
[
Gra74
, Section I,3.2]
for details.

Notation 2.6
.

In the following, an indexed category
\mathfrak{I}\colon\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
is denoted by
(\mathbb{X},\mathfrak{I})
, the category
\mathfrak{I}(A)
associated with an object
A
of
\mathbb{X}
is denoted by
\mathbb{X}^{\left(A\right)}
, the substitution functor
\mathfrak{I}(f)
induced by a morphism
f\colon A\to A^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}
is denoted by
f^{\ast}\colon\mathbb{X}^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}\to\mathbb{X}^{\left(A\right)}
,and the unitor and the compositor of
\mathfrak{I}
are denoted by
\mathfrak{I}^{0}
and
\mathfrak{I}^{2}
, respectively. Moreover, when the symbol used to denote an indexed category is decorated with a subscript or with a superscript, the same decoration is applied to the categories and the substitution functors. For example, for an indexed category
(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square},\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square})
, the corresponding categories are denoted by
\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\left(A\right)}
for each
A
of
\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}
, and the substitution functors are denoted by
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\ast}
. The same convention extends to the unitor and the compositor, e.g.,
\mathfrak{I}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}
and
\mathfrak{I}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}
.

Each cloven fibration
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\Pi)
is associated with an indexed category, denoted by
\mathscr{I}(\Pi)\colon\mathbb{X}^{\mathsf{op}}\to\mathsf{CAT}
, defined as follows:

categories.

Each object
A
of
\mathbb{X}
is associated with the corresponding fibre
\Pi^{-1}(A)
;

substitution functors.

Each morphism
f\colon A\to A^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}
is associated with the functor
f^{\ast}\colon\Pi^{-1}(A^{\prime})\to\Pi^{-1}(A)
which sends each object
E^{\prime}
over
A^{\prime}
to the object
f^{\ast}E^{\prime}
, domain of the cartesian lift
\varphi^{\left(f\right)}\colon f^{\ast}E^{\prime}\to E^{\prime}
of
f
, and which sends any vertical morphism
\xi\colon E^{\prime}\to E^{\prime\prime}
over
A^{\prime}
to the unique vertical morphism
f^{\ast}\xi\colon f^{\ast}E^{\prime}\to f^{\ast}E^{\prime\prime}
over
A
such that
\varphi^{\left(f\right)}_{E^{\prime\prime}}\circ f^{\ast}\xi=\varphi^{\left(f\right)}_{E^{\prime}}
, defined by the universality of the cartesian morphism
\varphi^{\left(f\right)}_{E^{\prime\prime}}
;

unitor.

Given an object
E
over
A
, the unitor is the unique vertical natural isomorphism
\mathfrak{I}^{0}\colon E\to\mathsf{id}_{A}^{\ast}E
such that
\mathfrak{I}^{0}\varphi^{\left(\mathsf{id}_{A}\right)}=\mathsf{id}_{E}
, induced by the universality of the cartesian morphism
\varphi^{\left(\mathsf{id}_{A}\right)}
;

compositor.

Given an object
E^{\prime\prime}
over
A^{\prime\prime}
and two morphisms
f\colon A\to A^{\prime}
and
g\colon A^{\prime}\to A^{\prime\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}
, the compositor is the unique vertical natural isomorphism
\mathfrak{I}^{2}\colon f^{\ast}g^{\ast}E^{\prime\prime}\to(g\circ f)^{\ast}E^{\prime\prime}
such that
\varphi^{\left(g\circ f\right)}\circ\mathfrak{I}^{2}=\varphi^{\left(g\right)}\circ\varphi^{\left(f\right)}
, induced by the universality of the cartesian morphism
\varphi^{\left(g\circ f\right)}
.

The process of “separating” a cloven fibration into the indexed category
\mathscr{I}(\Pi)
of its fibres is analogous to associating a fibre bundle
\pi\colon E\to M
over a topological space
M
with the assignment which sends each point
x
of
M
to the fibre
\pi^{-1}(x)
over
x
. This process can be reversed via the Grothendieck construction.

Let us start by considering an indexed category
(\mathbb{X},\mathfrak{I})
, which sends each object
A
of
\mathbb{X}
to the category
\mathbb{X}^{\left(A\right)}
and each morphism
f\colon A\to A^{\prime}
to the functor
f^{\ast}\colon\mathbb{X}^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}\to\mathbb{X}^{\left(A\right)}
. Then, the
category of elements
of
\mathfrak{I}
is the category
\mathsf{EL}(\mathbb{X},\mathfrak{I})
defined as follows:

objects.

The objects of
\mathsf{EL}(\mathbb{X},\mathfrak{I})
are pairs
(A,E)
formed by an object
A
of
\mathbb{X}
together with an object
E
of
\mathbb{X}^{\left(A\right)}
;

morphisms.

The morphisms of
\mathsf{EL}(\mathbb{X},\mathfrak{I})
are pairs
(f,\xi^{\left(f\right)})\colon(A,E)\to(A^{\prime},E^{\prime})
formed by a morphism
f\colon A\to A^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}
together with a morphism
\xi^{\left(f\right)}\colon E\to f^{\ast}E^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}^{\left(A\right)}
;

identities.

The identity morphism of a pair
(A,E)\in\mathsf{EL}(\mathbb{X},\mathfrak{I})
is given by the pair
(\mathsf{id}_{A},\xi^{\left(\mathsf{id}_{A}\right)})
, where
\xi^{\left(\mathsf{id}_{A}\right)}\colon E\to\mathsf{id}_{A}^{\ast}E
is the unitor of
\mathfrak{I}
;

composition.

Given two composable morphisms
(f,\xi^{\left(f\right)})\colon(A,E)\to(A^{\prime},E^{\prime})
and
(g,\xi^{\left(g\right)})\colon(A^{\prime},E^{\prime})\to(A^{\prime\prime},E^{\prime\prime})
of
\mathsf{EL}(\mathbb{X},\mathfrak{I})
, their composition is the pair
(g\circ f,\xi^{\left(g\circ f\right)})
where

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(g\circ f\right)}\colon E\xrightarrow{\xi^{\left(f\right)}}f^{\ast}E^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f^{\ast}\xi^{\left(g\right)}}f^{\ast}g^{\ast}E^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{I}^{2}}(g\circ f)^{\ast}E^{\prime\prime}

\mathfrak{I}^{2}
denoting the compositor of
\mathfrak{I}
.

The functor
\mathscr{F}(\mathfrak{I})\colon\mathsf{EL}(\mathbb{X},\mathfrak{I})\to\mathbb{X}
which sends each object
(A,E)
of
\mathsf{EL}(\mathbb{X},\mathfrak{I})
to the object
A
of
\mathbb{X}
and each morphism
(f,\xi^{\left(f\right)})
of
\mathsf{EL}(\mathbb{X},\mathfrak{I})
to the morphism
f
of
\mathbb{X}
is a cloven fibration.

The assignments
\mathscr{I}
and
\mathscr{F}
which send a cloven fibration to an indexed category and vice versa, respectively, extend to an equivalence of
2
-categories. To see this, let us recall the definition of the
1
-morphisms and
2
-morphisms of indexed categories and fibrations.

Definition 2.7
.

A
1
-morphism of cloven fibrations

(F,F^{\prime})\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square},\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\prime},\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\square})\to(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare},\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}^{\prime},\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare})
consists of a pair of functors
F\colon\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}\to\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}
and
F^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}^{\prime}
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

Moreover,
(F,F^{\prime})
is required to preserve the universality of the cartesian lifts defined by the cleavages of the fibrations. Concretely, this last condition means that, given a morphism
f\colon A\to A^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}
and an object
E^{\prime}
of the fibre over
A^{\prime}
w.r.t.
\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}
, the unique morphism
\kappa^{\left(f\right)}\colon F^{\prime}f_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\ast}(E^{\prime})\to(Ff)_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}^{\ast}(F^{\prime}E^{\prime})
making the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}(f_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\ast}E^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}(F^{\prime}E^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}(E^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\left(f\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\varphi^{\left(f\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(Ff\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}}

is an isomorphism, where
\varphi^{\left(f\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}\colon f_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\ast}E^{\prime}\to E^{\prime}
and
\varphi^{\left(Ff\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\colon(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}(F^{\prime}E^{\prime})\to F^{\prime}E^{\prime}
are the cartesian lifts of
f
on
E^{\prime}
w.r.t.
\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}
and the cartesian lift of
Ff
on
F^{\prime}E^{\prime}
w.r.t.
\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}
, respectively. The isomorphisms
\kappa^{\left(f\right)}
, indexed by the morphisms
f
of
\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}
, are called the
distributors
of the morphism
(F,F^{\prime})
.

Remark 2.8
.

The name
distributor
was adopted in analogy with distributive laws and it should not be confused with the notion of profunctors.

Definition 2.9
.

A
2
-morphism of fibrations

(\theta,\theta^{\prime})\colon(F,F^{\prime})\to(G,G^{\prime})
between two
1
-morphisms
(F,F^{\prime}),\linebreak(G,G^{\prime})\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square},\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\prime},\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\square})\to(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare},\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}^{\prime},\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare})
of fibrations consists of two natural transformations
\theta\colon F\Rightarrow G
and
\theta^{\prime}\colon F^{\prime}\Rightarrow G^{\prime}
such that:

\displaystyle\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\theta^{\prime}=\theta_{\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}

Notation 2.10
.

We denote by
\mathsf{FIB}
the
2
-category of cloven fibrations, and corresponding
1
-morphisms and
2
-morphisms. Given a category
\mathbb{X}
, we denote by
\mathsf{FIB}(\mathbb{X})
the
2
-category of cloven fibrations whose base category is
\mathbb{X}
,
1
-morphisms are
1
-morphisms of fibrations whose base functor
F\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
is the identity functor, and
2
-morphisms are
2
-morphisms of fibrations whose base natural transformation
\theta\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\Rightarrow\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}
is the identity.

Definition 2.11
.

A
1
-morphism of indexed categories

(F,F^{\prime},\kappa)\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square},\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square})\to(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare},\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare})
consists of a functor
F\colon\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}\to\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}
, a collection
F^{\prime}
of functors
F^{\left(A\right)}\colon\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\left(A\right)}\to\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}^{\left(FA\right)}
indexed by the objects
A
of
\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}
, from the category
\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\left(A\right)}\colon\kern-4.30554pt=\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}(A)
to the category
\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}^{\left(FA\right)}\colon\kern-4.30554pt=\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}(FA)
, and a collection
\kappa
of natural isomorphisms
\kappa^{\left(f\right)}\colon F^{\left(A\right)}\circ f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}\Rightarrow(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\circ F^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}
called
distributors
, indexed by the morphisms
f\colon A\to A^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}
, where
f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}\colon\kern-4.30554pt=\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}(f)
and
(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\colon\kern-4.30554pt=\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}(Ff)
. Moreover, the distributors are compatible with the unitors
\mathfrak{I}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square},\mathfrak{I}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}
and the compositors
\mathfrak{I}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square},\mathfrak{I}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}
of
\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}
, respectively, as follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\left(A\right)}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(F\mathsf{id}_{A})^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A\right)}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\left(A\right)}\mathsf{id}_{A{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}^{\ast}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathsf{id}_{FA})^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A\right)}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{I}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\left(A\right)}\mathfrak{I}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\left(\mathsf{id}_{A}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\left(A\right)}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}g^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}g^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}(Fg)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A^{\prime\prime}\right)}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\left(A\right)}(g\circ f)^{\ast}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(F(g\circ f))^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A^{\prime\prime}\right)}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(FfFg)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A^{\prime\prime}\right)}E^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\left(f\right)}g^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\left(A\right)}\mathfrak{I}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\kappa^{\left(g\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{I}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A^{\prime\prime}\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\left(g\circ f\right)}}

for every object
A
of
\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}
, morphisms
f\colon A\to A^{\prime}
and
g\colon A^{\prime}\to A^{\prime\prime}
of
\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}
, and objects
E,E^{\prime}
, and
E^{\prime\prime}
objects of
\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\prime}
over
A,A^{\prime}
, and
A^{\prime\prime}
, respectively.

Definition 2.12
.

A
2
-morphism of indexed categories

(\theta,\theta^{\prime})\colon(F,F^{\prime},\kappa)\Rightarrow(G,G^{\prime},\lambda)
between two
1
-morphisms
(F,F^{\prime},\kappa),(G,G^{\prime},\lambda)\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square},\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square})\to(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare},\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare})
consists of a natural transformation
\theta\colon F\Rightarrow G
and a collection of natural transformations
\theta^{\left(A\right)}\colon F^{\left(A\right)}\Rightarrow\theta^{\ast}\circ G^{\left(A\right)}
satisfying the following condition:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\left(A\right)}\circ f_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\ast}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta^{\ast}\circ G^{\left(A\right)}\circ f_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\ast}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta^{\ast}\circ(Gf)_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}^{\ast}\circ G^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}^{\ast}\circ F^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}^{\ast}\circ\theta^{\ast}\circ G^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\left(A\right)}f_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\left(f\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\ast}\lambda^{\left(f\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{I}^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Ff)_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}^{\ast}\theta^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}}

Notation 2.13
.

We denote by
\mathsf{INDX}
the
2
-category of indexed categories and corresponding
1
-morphisms and
2
-morphisms. Given a category
\mathbb{X}
, we denote by
\mathsf{INDX}(\mathbb{X})
the
2
-category of indexed categories whose base category is
\mathbb{X}
,
1
-morphisms are
1
-morphisms of indexed categories whose base functor
F\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
is the identity functor, and
2
-morphisms are
2
-morphisms of indexed categories whose base natural transformation
\theta\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\to\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}
is the identity.

The Grothendieck construction is usually stated in terms of an equivalence between cloven fibrations and indexed categories over a fixed base category
\mathbb{X}
. We suggest the reader to consult
[
Bor94
, Section 8.3]
. For the purposes of this paper, we need to extend this equivalence between cloven fibrations and indexed categories over any given base category.

Remark 2.14
.

In the literature, indexed categories are also described as pseudofunctors from the
1
-category
\mathsf{CAT}
of categories to the
2
-category
\mathsf{CAT}
of categories. Similarly,
1
-morphisms of indexed categories are known as pseudonatural transformations, and
2
-morphisms of indexed categories as modifications.

Proposition 2.15
.

The assignment
\mathscr{F}
which sends an indexed category
(\mathbb{X},\mathfrak{I})
to the cloven fibration
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{EL}(\mathbb{X},\mathfrak{I}),\mathscr{F}(\mathfrak{I}))
and the assignment
\mathscr{I}
which sends a cloven fibration
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\Pi)
to the indexed category
(\mathbb{X},\mathscr{I}(\Pi))
extend to an equivalence of
2
-categories:

\displaystyle\mathsf{FIB}\simeq\mathsf{INDX}

Proof.

Let us start by considering a
1
-morphism of fibrations
(F,F^{\prime})\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square},\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\prime},\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\square})\to(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare},\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}^{\prime},\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare})
and let us define the corresponding
1
-morphism of indexed categories. The base functor is
F\colon\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}\to\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}
. The collection of functors
F^{\left(A\right)}\colon\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{-1}(A)\to\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}^{-1}(FA)
is given by the restriction of
F^{\prime}
to the fibres
\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{-1}(A)
of
\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}
. Indeed, since
F\circ\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}=\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\circ F^{\prime}
, each
E\in\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{-1}(A)
is sent to
F^{\prime}E\in\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}^{-1}(FA)
by
F^{\prime}
. The distributor
\kappa^{\left(f\right)}\colon F^{\left(A\right)}\circ f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}\Rightarrow(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\circ F^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}
is defined by the distributor

\displaystyle\kappa^{\left(f\right)}\colon F^{\prime}(f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime})\to(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}(F^{\prime}E^{\prime})

induced by the universality of the cartesian lift
\varphi^{\left(Ff\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\colon(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}(F^{\prime}E^{\prime})\to F^{\prime}(f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime})
. To prove that
\kappa^{\left(f\right)}
is natural observe that, for a vertical morphism
\psi\colon E^{\prime}\to E^{\prime\prime}
, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}(f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}(f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}(F^{\prime}E^{\prime\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}(F^{\prime}E^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}\varphi^{\left(f\right)}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}(f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}\psi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\left(f\right)}_{E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(Ff\right)}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}E^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\left(f\right)}_{E^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(Ff\right)}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}E^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}(F^{\prime}\psi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}\varphi^{\left(f\right)}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}\psi}

So, in particular:

\displaystyle\varphi^{\left(Ff\right)}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}E^{\prime\prime}}\circ\kappa^{\left(f\right)}_{E^{\prime\prime}}\circ F^{\prime}(f_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\ast}\psi)=\varphi^{\left(Ff\right)}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}E^{\prime\prime}}\circ(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}(F^{\prime}\psi)\circ\kappa^{\left(f\right)}_{E^{\prime}}

From the universality of the cartesian lift
\varphi^{\left(Ff\right)}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}E^{\prime\prime}}
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\kappa^{\left(f\right)}_{E^{\prime\prime}}\circ F^{\prime}(f_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\ast}\psi)=(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}(F^{\prime}\psi)\circ\kappa^{\left(f\right)}_{E^{\prime}}

Similarly, to prove the compatibility between the unitors, compositors and distributors, first notice that the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}\mathsf{id}_{A{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}^{\ast}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathsf{id}_{FA})^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathsf{id}_{FA})^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}\mathfrak{I}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{I}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\left(\mathsf{id}_{A}\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}\varphi^{\left(\mathsf{id}_{A}\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(\mathsf{id}_{FA}\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(\mathsf{id}_{FA}\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}g^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\prime}g^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}(Fg)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\prime}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(F(g\circ f))^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\prime}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}g^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Fg)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\prime}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}(g\circ f)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(F(g\circ f))^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\prime}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}E^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}\circ\kappa^{\left(f\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}\varphi^{\left(f\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}g^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}\mathfrak{I}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\kappa^{\left(g\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(Ff\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\prime}g^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{I}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(Ff\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}(Fg)^{\ast}F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(F(g\circ f)\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\left(g\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}\varphi^{\left(g\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(Fg\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\left(g\circ f\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(F(g\circ f)\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}}

From the universality of the cartesian lifts we conclude:

\displaystyle\kappa^{\left(\mathsf{id}_{A}\right)}\circ F^{\left(A\right)}\mathfrak{I}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}={\mathfrak{I}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}}_{F^{\left(A\right)}}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}^{2}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A^{\prime\prime}\right)}}\circ(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\kappa^{\left(g\right)}\circ\kappa^{\left(f\right)}_{g^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}=\kappa^{\left(g\circ f\right)}\circ F^{\left(A\right)}\mathfrak{I}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}

Let us now consider a
2
-morphism
(\theta,\theta^{\prime})\colon(F,F^{\prime})\Rightarrow(G,G^{\prime})
of fibrations between two
1
-morphisms
(F,F^{\prime}),\linebreak(G,G^{\prime})\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square},\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\prime},\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\square})\to(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare},\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}^{\prime},\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare})
of fibrations and let us define the corresponding
2
-morphism

\displaystyle\mathscr{I}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})\colon\mathscr{I}(F,F^{\prime})\to\mathscr{I}(G,G^{\prime})

of indexed categories between the two
1
-morphisms
(F,F^{\prime},\kappa),(G,G^{\prime},\lambda)\colon\mathscr{I}(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square},\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\prime},\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\square})\to\mathscr{I}(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare},\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}^{\prime},\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare})
. The base natural transformation is
\theta\colon F\Rightarrow G
. To define the natural transformations
\theta^{\left(A\right)}\colon F^{\left(A\right)}\Rightarrow\theta^{\ast}\circ G^{\left(A\right)}
, first notice that, since
\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\theta^{\prime}=\theta_{\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
,
\theta^{\prime}\colon F^{\prime}E\to G^{\prime}E
is over
\theta\colon FA\to GA
, for
A=\Pi_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E
. Therefore, the universality of the cartesian lift
\varphi^{\left(\theta\right)}\colon\theta^{\ast}G^{\prime}E\to G^{\prime}E
defines a unique morphism
\theta^{\left(A\right)}_{E}\colon F^{\prime}E\to\theta^{\ast}G^{\prime}E
making the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta^{\ast}G^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\left(A\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(\theta\right)}}

To show that
\theta^{\left(A\right)}
is natural, consider a vertical morphism
\psi\colon E\to E^{\prime}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta^{\ast}G^{\prime}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta^{\ast}G^{\prime}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\left(A\right)}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}_{E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\left(A\right)}_{E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(\theta\right)}_{E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\ast}G^{\prime}\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(\theta\right)}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}\psi}

In particular

\displaystyle\varphi^{\left(\theta\right)}_{E^{\prime}}\circ\theta^{\ast}G^{\prime}\psi\circ\theta^{\left(A\right)}_{E}=\varphi^{\left(\theta\right)}_{E^{\prime}}\circ\theta^{\left(A\right)}_{E^{\prime}}\circ F^{\prime}\psi

and from the universality of
\varphi^{\left(\theta\right)}_{E^{\prime}}\colon\theta^{\ast}G^{\prime}E^{\prime}\to G^{\prime}E^{\prime}
we deduce:

\displaystyle\theta^{\ast}G^{\prime}\psi\circ\theta^{\left(A\right)}_{E}=\theta^{\left(A\right)}_{E^{\prime}}\circ F^{\prime}\psi

To prove the compatibility between the distributors and
\theta^{\prime}
, observe that the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta^{\ast}G^{\prime}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta^{\ast}(Gf)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}G^{\prime}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\theta^{\ast}G^{\prime}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Gf)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}G^{\prime}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta^{\ast}G^{\prime}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\left(A\right)}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\ast}\lambda^{\left(f\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(\theta\right)}G^{\prime}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(\theta\right)}(Gf)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}G^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(Ff\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\theta^{\ast}G^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{\left(f\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}\varphi^{\left(f\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(Ff\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}G^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(\theta\right)}G^{\prime}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\prime}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\theta^{\ast}G^{\prime}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\prime}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta^{\ast}G^{\prime}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\prime}E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\left(f\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}\varphi^{\left(f\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\theta^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(Ff\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(Ff\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\theta^{\ast}G^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\left(\theta\right)}G^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}\varphi^{\left(f\right)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}

So, we have

\displaystyle\varphi^{\left(\theta\right)}_{G^{\prime}}\circ\varphi^{\left(Ff\right)}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\theta^{\ast}G^{\prime}}\circ\theta^{\ast}\lambda^{\left(f\right)}\circ\theta^{\left(A\right)}_{f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}=\varphi^{\left(\theta\right)}_{G^{\prime}}\circ\varphi^{\left(Ff\right)}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\theta^{\ast}G^{\prime}}\circ(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\theta^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}\circ\kappa^{\left(f\right)}

and, from the universality of the cartesian lifts, we obtain

\displaystyle\theta^{\ast}\lambda^{\left(f\right)}\circ\theta^{\left(A\right)}_{f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}=(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\theta^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}\circ\kappa^{\left(f\right)}

which is precisely the compatibility between
\theta^{\prime}
and the distributors.

Conversely, consider a
1
-morphism
(F,F^{\prime},\kappa)\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square},\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square})\to(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare},\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare})
of indexed categories and let us define the corresponding
1
-morphism of fibrations. Let us start with the base functor, which is just the base functor
F\colon\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}\to\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}
. To define the total functor
F^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}^{\prime}
, let us consider an object
(A,E)\in\mathsf{EL}(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square},\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square})
and let us define

\displaystyle F^{\prime}(A,E)\colon\kern-4.30554pt=(FA,F^{\left(A\right)}E)

where
F^{\left(A\right)}\colon\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}^{\left(A\right)}\to\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}^{\left(FA\right)}
, so
(FA,F^{\left(A\right)}E)
is indeed an object of
\mathsf{EL}(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare},\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare})
. Now, consider a morphism
(f,\xi^{\left(f\right)})\colon(A,E)\to(A^{\prime},E^{\prime})
of
\mathsf{EL}(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square},\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square})
. Let us define
F^{\prime}(f,\xi^{\left(f\right)})
as the morphism
(Ff,\xi^{\left(Ff\right)})
, where

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(Ff\right)}\colon F^{\left(A\right)}E\xrightarrow{F^{\left(A\right)}\xi^{\left(f\right)}}F^{\left(A\right)}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\kappa^{\left(f\right)}}(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}E^{\prime}

where we employed the distributor
\kappa^{\left(f\right)}\colon F^{\left(A\right)}\circ f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}\Rightarrow(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\circ F^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}
. To prove that
F^{\left(A\right)}
is functorial, we need to employ the compatibilities of the distributors with the unitors and the compositors. Let us start by showing that
F^{\prime}\mathsf{id}_{(A,E)}=\mathsf{id}_{F^{\prime}(A,E)}
:

\displaystyle F^{\prime}\left(\mathsf{id}_{(A,E)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F^{\prime}\left(\mathsf{id}_{A},\mathfrak{I}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}\colon E\to\mathsf{id}_{A{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}^{\ast}E\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(F\mathsf{id}_{A},F^{\left(A\right)}E\xrightarrow{F^{\left(A\right)}\mathfrak{I}^{0}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}F^{\left(A\right)}\mathsf{id}_{A{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}^{\ast}E\xrightarrow{\kappa^{\left(\mathsf{id}_{A}\right)}}(F\mathsf{id}_{A})^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A\right)}E\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{id}_{FA},\mathfrak{I}^{0}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A\right)}}\colon F^{\left(A\right)}E\to(\mathsf{id}_{FA})^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A\right)}E\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{id}_{F^{\prime}(A,E)}

Consider now two morphisms
(f,\xi^{\left(f\right)})\colon(A,E)\to(A^{\prime},E^{\prime})
and
(g,\xi^{\left(g\right)})\colon(A^{\prime},E^{\prime})\to(A^{\prime\prime},E^{\prime\prime})
of
\mathsf{EL}(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square},\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square})
. First, recall that
F^{\prime}(f,\xi^{\left(f\right)})=(Ff,\xi^{\left(Ff\right)})
and
F^{\prime}(g,\xi^{\left(g\right)})=(Fg,\xi^{\left(Fg\right)})
, where:

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(Ff\right)}\colon F^{\left(A\right)}E\xrightarrow{F^{\left(A\right)}\xi^{\left(f\right)}}F^{\left(A\right)}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\kappa^{\left(f\right)}}(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}E^{\prime}

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(Fg\right)}\colon F^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}E^{\prime}\xrightarrow{F^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}\xi^{\left(g\right)}}F^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}g^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\kappa^{\left(g\right)}}(Fg)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A^{\prime\prime}\right)}E^{\prime\prime}

Moreover:

\displaystyle\left(g,\xi^{\left(g\right)}\right)\circ\left(f,\xi^{\left(f\right)}\right)=\left(g\circ f,E\xrightarrow{\xi^{\left(f\right)}}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}\xi^{\left(g\right)}}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}g^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{I}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}(g\circ f)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime\prime}\right)

Therefore,
F^{\prime}\left(\left(g,\xi^{\left(g\right)}\right)\circ\left(f,\xi^{\left(f\right)}\right)\right)=\left(F(g\circ f),\xi^{\left(F(g\circ f)\right)}\right)
, where:

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(F(g\circ f)\right)}\colon F^{\left(A\right)}E\xrightarrow{F^{\left(A\right)}\xi^{\left(f\right)}}F^{\left(A\right)}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime}\xrightarrow{F^{\left(A\right)}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}\xi^{\left(g\right)}}F^{\left(A\right)}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}g^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{F^{\left(A\right)}\mathfrak{I}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}F^{\left(A\right)}(g\circ f)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\kappa^{\left(g\circ f\right)}}(F(g\circ f))^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A^{\prime\prime}\right)}E^{\prime\prime}

However, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\left(A\right)}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\left(A\right)}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}g^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\left(A\right)}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}g^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}(Fg)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A^{\prime\prime}\right)}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\left(A\right)}(g\circ f)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\left(A\right)}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(F(g\circ f))^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A^{\prime\prime}\right)}E^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\left(A\right)}\xi^{\left(f\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\left(f\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\left(A\right)}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}\xi^{\left(g\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}\xi^{\left(g\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\kappa^{\left(g\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\left(f\right)}g^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\left(A\right)}\mathfrak{I}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{I}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A^{\prime\prime}\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\left(g\circ f\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\left(A\right)}\xi^{\left(g\circ f\right)}}

Therefore:

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(F(g\circ f),\xi^{\left(F(g\circ f)\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(F(g\circ f),\kappa^{\left(g\circ f\right)}\circ F^{\left(A\right)}\mathfrak{I}^{2}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}\circ F^{\left(A\right)}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}\xi^{\left(g\right)}\circ F^{\left(A\right)}\xi^{\left(f\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(Fg\circ Ff,\mathfrak{I}^{2}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A^{\prime\prime}\right)}}\circ(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\kappa^{\left(g\right)}\circ(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}\xi^{\left(g\right)}\circ\kappa^{\left(f\right)}\circ F^{\left(A\right)}\xi^{\left(f\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(Fg,\xi^{\left(Fg\right)}\right)\circ\left(Ff,\xi^{\left(Ff\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F^{\prime}\left(g,\xi^{\left(g\right)}\right)\circ F^{\prime}\left(f,\xi^{\left(f\right)}\right)

Let us now consider a
2
-morphism
(\theta,\theta^{\prime})\colon(F,F^{\prime},\kappa)\Rightarrow(G,G^{\prime},\lambda)
of indexed categories between two
1
-morphisms
(F,F^{\prime},\kappa),(G,G^{\prime},\lambda)\colon(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square},\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square})\to(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare},\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare})
of indexed categories. Let us define the corresponding
2
-morphism of fibrations
\mathscr{F}(\theta,\theta^{\prime})\colon\mathscr{F}(F,F^{\prime},\kappa)\Rightarrow\mathscr{F}(G,G^{\prime},\lambda)
. First, the base natural transformation is given by
\theta\colon F\Rightarrow G
. The total natural transformation
\theta^{\prime}\colon F^{\prime}\to G^{\prime}
is the morphism so defined:

\displaystyle(\theta^{\prime}\colon F^{\prime}(A,E)=(FA,F^{\left(A\right)}E)\to(GA,G^{\left(A\right)}E)=G^{\prime}(A,E))=(\theta\colon FA\to GA,\theta^{\left(A\right)}\colon F^{\left(A\right)}E\to\theta^{\ast}G^{\left(A\right)}E)

Let us prove that
\theta^{\prime}
is natural. Consider a morphism
(f,\xi^{\left(f\right)})\colon(A,E)\to(A^{\prime},E^{\prime})
of
\mathsf{EL}(\mathbb{X}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square},\mathfrak{I}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square})
. Then

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\left(A\right)}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\left(A\right)}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}F^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Ff)_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}^{\ast}\theta^{\ast}G^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta^{\ast}G^{\left(A\right)}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta^{\ast}G^{\left(A\right)}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\theta^{\ast}(Gf)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}G^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\left(A\right)}\xi^{\left(f\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\left(A\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{\left(f\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\left(A\right)}f^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Ff)^{\ast}_{\scriptscriptstyle\blacksquare}\theta^{\left(A^{\prime}\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\ast}G^{\left(A\right)}\xi^{\left(f\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\ast}\lambda^{\left(f\right)}}

where we used the compatibility of
\theta^{\prime}
with the distributors. This shows that
\mathscr{I}\colon\mathsf{FIB}\to\mathsf{INDX}
and
\mathscr{F}\colon\mathsf{INDX}\to\mathsf{FIB}
are
2
-functors. We leave to the reader to show that they form an equivalence, since this is just an extension to the classical equivalence
\mathsf{FIB}(\mathbb{X})\simeq\mathsf{INDX}(\mathbb{X})
.
∎

2.2
Tangent category theory

Tangent category theory aims to categorically axiomatize some of the fundamental constructions of differential geometry. In particular, a tangent category, first introduced by Rosickỳ in
[
Ros84
]
and further generalized by Cockett and Cruttwell in
[
CC14
]
, consists of a category equipped with an endofunctor which can be interpreted as the tangent bundle functor. An object in a tangent category can be interpreted as a geometric space equipped with a tangent bundle which encodes a notion of
local linearity
for this space. Informally, the fibres of the tangent bundle can be interpreted as
tangent spaces
of the given space, i.e., the best
local linear
approximation of the space at a given point.

The notion of linearity in a tangent category is not an intrinsic notion, but rather a contextual notion: the tangent structure establishes what it means for a morphism to be linear. In
[
CC18
]
, Cockett and Cruttwell introduced the concept of differential bundles in a tangent category, which are the analogs of vector bundles in the category of smooth manifolds, as shown by MacAdam in
[
Mac21
]
. Differential bundles represent precisely those fibre bundle-like objects whose fibres carry a linear structure.

In this section, we would like to recall the main definitions of a tangent category, of a differential bundle in a tangent category and discuss some examples.

We start with the definition of a tangent category. We suggest the reader to consult
[
CC14
]
. The original definition of Rosickỳ presented in
[
Ros84
]
is now known with the denomination of a
tangent category with negatives
, sometimes also called a
Rosickỳ tangent category
(see
[
CL23
]
or
[
ILL24
]
). First, let us recall the definition of an additive bundle.

Definition 2.16
.

An
additive bundle
of a category
\mathbb{X}
is a commutative monoid internal to the slice category
\mathbb{X}/B
for a given object
B
of
\mathbb{X}
. Concretely, this consists of a morphism
q\colon E\to B
of
\mathbb{X}
, called the
projection
, for which the
n
-fold pullback
E_{n}\to B
of
q
along itself exists, together with a morphism
z_{q}\colon B\to E
of
q
, called the
zero morphism
, and a morphism
s_{q}\colon E_{2}\to E
, called the
sum morphism
, satisfying the following properties:

\star

z_{q}
is a section of
q
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}}

\star

s_{q}
is a bundle morphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

\pi_{k}\colon E_{n}\to E
being the
k
-th projection of the
n
-fold pullback;

\star

Associativity:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{E}\times_{A}s_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{q}\times_{A}\mathsf{id}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{q}}

\star

Unitality:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle qz_{q},\mathsf{id}_{E}\rangle}

\star

Commutativity:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{q}}

where
\tau\colon E_{2}\to E_{2}
denotes the flip
\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{1}\rangle
.

An
additive bundle morphism
consists of a pair
(f,g)\colon(B,E,q,z_{q},s_{q})\to(B^{\prime},E^{\prime},q^{\prime},z_{q}^{\prime},s_{q}^{\prime})
of morphisms
f\colon B\to B^{\prime}
and
g\colon E\to E^{\prime}
satisfying the following properties:

\star

Compatibility with the projections:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

\star

Compatibility with the zero morphisms:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z_{q}^{\prime}}

\star

Additivity:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\times_{B}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{q}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Definition 2.17
.

A
tangent category
is a category
\mathbb{X}
equipped with a
tangent structure
, which consists of the following data:

tangent bundle functor.

An endofunctor
\mathrm{T}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
, called the
tangent bundle functor
;

projection.

A natural transformation
p\colon\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}
, called the
projection
, for which the
n
-fold pullbacks
\mathrm{T}_{n}A\to A
of
p\colon\mathrm{T}A\to A
along itself exist and preserved by the functors
\mathrm{T}^{m}\colon\kern-4.30554pt=\mathrm{T}\circ\mathrm{T}\circ{\dots}\circ\mathrm{T}
, for every positive integer
m
(for
m=1
,
\mathrm{T}^{1}\colon\kern-4.30554pt=\mathrm{T}
). The
k
-th projection is denoted by
\pi_{k}\colon\mathrm{T}_{n}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
;

zero morphism.

A natural transformation
z\colon\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{X}}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
, called the
zero morphism
;

sum morphism.

A natural transformation
s\colon\mathrm{T}_{2}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
, called the
sum morphism
;

vertical lift.

A natural transformation
l\colon\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{2}
, called the
vertical lift
;

canonical flip.

A natural transformation
c\colon\mathrm{T}^{2}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}^{2}
, called the
canonical flip
;

satisfying the following properties:

\star

Additive structure. For each object
A\in\mathbb{X}
,
(A,\mathrm{T}A,p,z,s)
is an additive bundle;

\star

Additivity of vertical lift. For each object
A\in\mathbb{X}

\displaystyle(z,l)\colon(A,\mathrm{T}A,p,z,s)\to(\mathrm{T}A,\mathrm{T}^{2}A,\mathrm{T}p,\mathrm{T}z,\mathrm{T}s)

is an additive bundle morphism;

\star

Additivity of canonical flip. For each object
A\in\mathbb{X}

\displaystyle(\mathsf{id}_{\mathrm{T}A},c)\colon(\mathrm{T}A,\mathrm{T}^{2}A,\mathrm{T}p,\mathrm{T}z,\mathrm{T}s)\to(\mathrm{T}A,\mathrm{T}^{2}A,p_{\mathrm{T}},z_{\mathrm{T}},s_{\mathrm{T}})

is an additive bundle morphism;

\star

Coassociativity of vertical lift:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l\mathrm{T}}

\star

The canonical flip is a symmetric braiding:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\mathrm{T}}

\star

Compatibility between canonical flip and vertical lift:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{3}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\mathrm{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}l}

\star

Universality of the vertical lift. The following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}^{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}

is a pullback diagram, where:

\displaystyle\phi\colon\mathrm{T}_{2}A\xrightarrow{l\times_{A}z_{\mathrm{T}}}\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}_{2}A\xrightarrow{\mathrm{T}s}\mathrm{T}^{2}A

A tangent category
has negatives
when it comes equipped with:

negation.

A natural transformation
n\colon\mathrm{T}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
, called
negation
, such that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}_{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{T}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathsf{id},n\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}

The tangent bundle functor sends an object
A
to its tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}A
, which is interpreted as the space of tangent vectors of
A
at each point. Moreover, it sends a morphism
f\colon A\to A^{\prime}
, regarded as a smooth map, to the morphism
\mathrm{T}f
which sends each tangent vector
u
at a given point
x
to the differential of
f
at
x
applied to
u
, i.e.,
\mathrm{d}f_{x}(u)
. The functoriality of
\mathrm{T}
corresponds to the chain rule of the differential.

The projection, the zero morphism, and the sum morphism equip each tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}A\to A
with an additive structure. Informally, this structure axiomatizes the additive structure of the tangent spaces of a tangent bundle of a smooth manifold. By interpreting
\mathrm{T}A
as the space of vector tangent vectors over each point of
A
, the projection sends each tangent vector to its base point, the zero morphism associates each point with the zero tangent vector, and the sum morphism allows one to sum together two tangent vectors with the same base point.

When the tangent category has negatives, the additive structure over the fibres becomes a commutative group. This can be interpreted as inverting the orientation of the tangent vectors.

The double tangent bundle
\mathrm{T}^{2}A
can be regarded as the space of infinitesimal differentiable homotopies of
A
, i.e., tangent vectors of tangent vectors. Locally, one can represent such an element as a quadruple
(x,u,v,\omega)
formed by a point
x
of
A
, two tangent vectors
u,v
of
A
at
x
, and a tangent vector
\omega
of
\mathrm{T}A
at
(x,u)
. Then, the vertical lift sends a tangent vector
u
of
A
at a given point
x
to
(x,0,0,u)
.

The universality of the vertical lift establishes that the vertical bundle, which is the bundle whose fibres are the kernel of the differential of the projection at each point, is trivial. This can be interpreted as a local linearity condition for the tangent bundle, that is, an object in a tangent category has a local linear behaviour.

Finally, the canonical flip encodes the symmetry of the Hessian matrix, i.e., the commutativity of partial derivatives for smooth functions.

Notation 2.18
.

For a tangent category
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
, we denote the tangent bundle functor using the same letter of the tangent structure
\mathbb{T}
, adopting the font
\mathrm{T}\colon\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}
. The
n
-fold pullback of the projection is denoted by
\mathrm{T}_{n}
with the corresponding projections denoted by
\pi_{k}\colon\mathrm{T}_{n}\Rightarrow\mathrm{T}
. The projection, the zero morphism, the sum morphism, the vertical lift, and the canonical flip are denoted with the letters
p,z,s,l
, and
c
, respectively; when
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{T})
has negatives, the negation is denoted by
n
. When the symbol of the tangent structure is decorated with subscripts or subscripts, the same decoration applies to
\mathrm{T},\mathrm{T}_{n},p,z,s,l,c
, and
n
whenever the tangent structure has negatives.

Example 2.19
.

Every category
\mathbb{X}
admits a trivial tangent structure, whose tangent bundle functor and whose natural transformations are the identities.

Example 2.20
.

The category of finite-dimensional smooth manifolds forms a tangent category with negatives whose tangent bundle functor is the usual notion of the tangent bundle functor in differential geometry.

Example 2.21
.

The category of commutative and unital rigs (i.e., rings without negatives, a.k.a. semirings)
\mathsf{CRIG}
has a canonical tangent category whose tangent bundle functor sends a rig
R
to the rig
R[\varepsilon]\colon\kern-4.30554pt=R[x]/(x^{2})
; the projection
p\colon R[\varepsilon]\to R
sends
a\in R
to itself; the zero morphism
z\colon R\to R[\varepsilon]
sends
\varepsilon
to
0
; the sum
s\colon R[\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}]\to R[\varepsilon]
sends both
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{2}
to
\varepsilon
, where
R[\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}]\colon\kern-4.30554pt=R[x_{1},x_{2}]/(x_{i}x_{j};i,j=1,2)
; the vertical lift
l\colon R[\varepsilon]\to R[\varepsilon][\varepsilon^{\prime}]
sends
\varepsilon
to
\varepsilon^{\prime}\varepsilon
, where
R[\varepsilon][\varepsilon^{\prime}]\colon\kern-4.30554pt=R[x,x^{\prime}]/(x^{2},{x^{\prime}}^{2})
; finally, the canonical flip
c\colon R[\varepsilon][\varepsilon^{\prime}]\to R[\varepsilon][\varepsilon^{\prime}]
sends
\varepsilon
to
\varepsilon^{\prime}
and vice versa.

A similar tangent structure is also defined in the category
\mathsf{CRING}
of commutative and unital rings. This tangent structure has negatives, whose negation
n\colon R[\varepsilon]\to R[\varepsilon]
sends
\varepsilon
to
-\varepsilon
.

Example 2.22
.

The opposite of the category of commutative and unital rigs comes equipped with a tangent structure, which is adjoint to the tangent structure of Example
2.21
, i.e., the tangent bundle functors form an adjunction. Concretely, the tangent bundle functor of
\mathsf{CRIG}^{\mathsf{op}}
sends a rig
R
to the symmetric rig of the module of Kähler differentials
\mathrm{T}R\colon\kern-4.30554pt=\mathrm{Sym}_{R}\Omega^{1}_{R/\mathbb{N}}
of
R
, i.e., to the rig generated by all elements
a
of
R
together with symbols
\mathrm{d}a
, for each
a
of
R
, satisfying the following relations:

\displaystyle a\cdot_{\mathrm{T}R}b=a\cdot_{R}b

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(1)=0

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(ra+sb)=r\mathrm{d}a+s\mathrm{d}b

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(ab)=b\mathrm{d}a+a\mathrm{d}b
